In other words, demanding bread and freedom, the protesters courageously cried out about the right to freedom and belief in God. I will remind you that at that time in Poland 2/3 of the diocese was uninhabited. Primate Stefan Wyszyński, Bishop Baraniak, Bishop Kaczmarek were imprisoned, others removed from the diocese, guarded by monasteries, many priests were imprisoned. Caritas was taken from the church, religious instruction was removed from the schools, there was no independent Catholic press, permanent trials, executions on the most faithful patriotic homelands. It is very important that the cognizants, demanding bread and freedom, did not only think of themselves, but remembered the whole nation and the Church. This is for this Polish solidarity, for calling for the subjectivity of the communists directed 2 armored divisions against the workers, 2 infantry divisions, in total over 10,000 soldiers with 360 tanks. Historians are still not sure if 57 people were killed or more and some 650 were injured. Among the killed, as was recalled here, there was a 13-year-old boy symbol, Romek Strzałkowski, who walked in the first row and carried a banner with a sign demanding the restoration of religion in the school. I would like to stress once again: it is very important that the audience demanded not only the improvement of living conditions and greater civil liberties, but also for the first time in post-war Poland that religious freedom was called for so massively. I mentioned that Poznań's break was occupied with victims, sufferings and persecutions, but this sacrifice of the cognizants was not in vain. It should be said that soon after Poznan June he could come and came Polish October. At that time, exactly on August 26th, on Jasna Góra the vows of the nation were renewed, trusting in the fate of the motherland of the Blessed Mother. The preparations for the celebration of the millennium of Poland's baptism began, Gomulka returned to power, the Primate and other bishops were released from Komancza, religion But only for 2 years, the school was restored. Mrs. Marshal, High Izbo, it must be added that on June 28, 1981, on the 20th anniversary of Poznań's June, on Adam Mickiewicz Square in Poznań, 2 monumental crosses were unveiled, as already stated in the resolution, connected by a joint cross-arm, attached by thick ties. Next to the ground is a pillar of the third cross, broken a few meters above the ground and crowned with an eagle breaking off for flight. It then wrote the words "For liberty, law, and bread." What is very important, 25 years later, in 2006, on the 50th anniversary of the events, workers welded the word "God" to Poznań crosses. It was only by adding this one word that the full truth of those events was given. The Archbishop, the Metropolitan of Poznań Stanisław Gądecki then said, without this expression, everything that is written on this monument is not quite true. Such questions on the margin: why was this reference to God not placed on Poznań crosses in 1981 when the monument was erected? Sometimes it takes time for the full historical truth to appear. Today we know very well that the acquaintances did not forget God at that time. By demanding bread and freedom, they courageously cried out for the right to religious freedom. In other words, the Protestants bore witness to a deeper view of the world. They testified that without God, without religion, without natural law, freedom is incomplete and even dangerous, because the laws established then do not have permanent foundations. Finally, I would like to ask myself and you: what remains of us from Poznań June, from this cry not only for bread but also for God? I'm asking you this in terms of the times we've lived to see. Today it is heroic to defend supposedly dying trees in the Białowieża Forest. It became heroic, as we saw recently in Warsaw on the streets, boasting of feminists during Manify, who killed unborn children many times. What about Romk Strzłkowski's peers who blasphemously threw them on the ground and trampled on the host? There are many reasons for such attitudes, and among them, that we do not respect our history, that young people in schools impose some slogan, indefinite vision of Polish history without responsibility for the future. Let us draw conclusions from Poznań's June or Gdańsk's August, that Polish freedom must not be separated from the belief that conscience and bread are equivalent to each other, constitute a human trait, determine our dignity and identity as Poles and Catholics. Thank you for your attention. [[Applause]] [[The meeting is chaired by Vice-Marshal Maria Koc]]

ParlaMint-PL_2016-06-16-senat-20-2.u369	Mrs. Marshal! Ladies and gentlemen of the Senators! As a result of the end of World War II, or actually as a result of the Yalta conference the Stalinist period began in Poland. It's a very difficult time. In 1949, when it was Stalin's birthday anniversary, the party authorities concluded that the patronage of Hippolite Cegielski's Plant should be changed to Józef Stalin. Also, as the senator mentioned, Adam Mickiewicz Square in Poznań was named Joseph Stalin. Ladies and gentlemen, this period has been a very difficult time. But then the year 1956 came, when the workers of Hippolit Cegielski's Departments were conducting talks, when the union workers from this plant were discussing the working conditions they had, about the progressive increases in tax that were to take place, about the hidden increases and many other demands they had made. They went to Warsaw, to the Ministry, they had conversations, but they didn't have any effect. And when they returned to Poznań, on June 28th at 6:00 a.m. on the first shift at the Department in 3 at the Hippolite Cegielski Works, the workers started protesting. They didn't take the job. The next departments stopped at 6:15, 6:30 p.m., the workers told everyone there was a strike that they weren't taking the job. And for the first time after 1945, the workers went out into the streets. We remember what happened in the meantime in Poland: referendum, fraudulent elections that took place. And now the workers are taking action to make a difference. They're the ones who've been burdened with this task. They go out on the streets and walk down Dzierżyńskiego Street That's her name. They split up into two groups. One group follows the streets of Workers' and Industrial Affairs, the other the People's Guard. This group goes to the Poznań Fair. It so happens that during that time the International Poznań Fair was held. In 1921 the Poznań Fair was established. In 1929 there was this big, famous Universal National Exhibition. Since then, many visitors have been coming to the Poznań Fair. There were a lot of foreigners at the fair, so it was known that this event could not be hidden. Already in various countries, in various European capitals, all information about what is happening in Poznań was provided. The crowd split up, but he came back, came back from one side and the other. Meanwhile, the residents of Poznań gathered, about 100,000 people gathered outside the building of the voivodship committee of the party. There was an attempt to hold talks with the President of the City Council of Poznań, with the committee, with representatives of the regional committee of the party. Unfortunately, the effect of these conversations was none. Therefore, the workers first tried and then entered the building of the voivodship committee of the party. There was also a rumor that workers' representatives were arrested, that one of the repair workers of the rolling stock repair plant... It was another factory, too, from which the workers on the way, as the workers walked. They joined this protest because he was arrested... And the password came: we go to the longest street where there is a prison in Poznań. And there was an entrance to the prison grounds, to the release of prisoners, after all, many political prisoners who were there. And so further action began in the city of Poznań. That's where the workers got some of the guns. Some of the workers went to Kochanowskiego Street, where the seat of the security office was. Of course, they also tried to enter the security office. The first shots fired on Kochanowskiego Street, and there were direct fights and clashes in the area of the city of Poznań. And that's where Romek Strzalkowski, the 13-year-old boy who first carried the flag, and then somewhere in the warehouse, was stopped, died. But there are many, many people who have been involved in these activities who have died. During this time, the first secretary of PZPR arrives in Poznań... sorry, the secretary of PZPR was Edward Gierek, who came to Poznań as well. The Prime Minister, Józef Cyrankiewicz, comes, the Minister, the Deputy Minister of the Interior Stanisław Popławski. The Popławski we talked about here, when we were debating street names, that they had to be decommunized. You know who Popławski was, a Soviet general, then a general in Poland, a Deputy Minister, he took command in Poznań when it comes to events of Poznan June. In Ławica initially both Józef Cyrankiewicz and Edward Gierek as well as Popławski had such a command center. Tanks were directed to Poznań at night. This view of all survivors of this Poznań June, they remember. They remember Wielkopolska Square, Marcinkowski Avenue, 359 tanks in the city of Poznań and ongoing battles. Such forces were used, such forces were drawn from the Silesian military district. It was difficult to win this Poznań Uprising in view of such overwhelming force. But the fight lasted 20... June 29th and June 30th. 29 June Prime Minister said That's what I was talking about. Whoever raises his hand to that hand will be torn off. These are the words of Józef Cyrankiewicz, which were uttered by him on the radio in Poznań. And unfortunately, the consequences of these events were many casualties. Various sources speak of various numbers: 57 victims, 58 victims, and seventy-seven victims. It is difficult to determine exactly this number, because they are people who died, but then for a few or several days, or even a few months there were further victims of these events. Ladies and gentlemen, we remember all this and that it was the first such a labour protest in Poland. In October 1956 we were also faced with an uprising in Hungary. Hungarians also thought, as it was in Poznań, that they could create an uprising that there would be no interference of military, Soviet forces. This did not happen in Poznań, but so many tanks were used against the inhabitants of Poznań that there was no chance. In 1956, as a result of this uprising, Poznan June, an uprising broke out in Hungary. And we must remember these events. In 1981, when Lech Wałęsa was the head of a very important event, the rise of the Solidarity... Throughout 1980 and 1981, until the time when martial law was declared, various actions were taken, as well as acquaintances remembered that there was a need to celebrate those events. Therefore, on 28 June 1981 a monument commemorating the victims of Poznań June was unveiled in Poznań, in the presence of Lech Wałęsa. And there are dates on this monument, which are important and which I mentioned, i.e. 1968, 1970, 1976, recently we adopted a resolution in this case. 1980 and 1981. This year is the 60th round anniversary of the events of 28 June 1956. Of course, the ceremony in Poznań will take place for this purpose, and we would like to celebrate the memory of all those who died who fought for freedom. Because that first Poznań Uprising, in Poznań, which has traditions when it comes to uprisings... We talked here, while talking about Ignacy Jan Paderewski, that he was also responsible for the fact that this uprising exploded. To conclude, ladies and gentlemen, we remember this and we want to commemorate it. ParlaMint-PL_2016-06-16-senat-20-2.u396	Mr. Marshal! High Izbo! I address my statement to two addressees, to the President of the Republic of Poland, Mr Andrzej Duda, and to the Minister of Foreign Affairs, Mr Witold Waszczykowski. Dear Mr. President, Dear Minister, 25 years ago, on 17 June 1991, a treaty was signed in Bonn between the Republic of Poland and the Federal Republic of Germany with good neighbourhood and friendly cooperation. They signed under it, from the Polish side, Jan Krzysztof Bielecki and Krzysztof Skubiszewski, from the German side of Helmut Kohl and Hans-Dietrich Genscher. These close to 25 years of the Treaty's validity It is a period of powerlessness, apparent actions, talkiness, lack of perception of real problems on the part of Poland, and domination, arrogance, hypocrisy and insolent exploitation of Polish weaknesses on the part of Germany. For 25 years the Republic of Poland has agreed to a clear asymmetry to its disadvantage in terms of both content and implementation of treaty provisions. As to the content of the Treaty, it is Article. Article 20 (1) (b) and (c) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 1 of the Treaty deals with the concept of German minority in the Republic of Poland and persons in the Federal Republic of Germany who have German nationality, which are of Polish origin or admit to the language, culture or Polish tradition. This lack of the concept of Polish minority in Germany in the Treaty already in itself demonstrates the unequal relations between Poland and Germany. International politics is a world of symbols. Inequality is a symbol of dependence and limitation of sovereignty. In addition, this inequality also has a negative effect on Poland and Poles, i.e. the absence of a Polish minority among the national and ethnic minorities recognised by the RFN under the Framework Convention for the Protection of National Minorities, and this in turn causes concrete negative phenomena: the lack of German support for Poles in Germany, while the privileges and means of protecting them are in Germany, based on this framework convention, groups such as the Danes, Serbo-Georgia, Frisians, Sinti and Roma. The second real negative effect is the refusal to restore the status of the Polish national minority in Germany, which it lost in 1940 on the basis of discriminatory Nazi law, so-called. Order of Göring. The German Government is now maintaining the effects of the law adopted by the Third Reich and does not recognise the Polish minority on its basis, nor does it return the property plundered by the Nazis. This outrageous position is justified by the fact that Poles with historical pre-war roots, which is not true, do not live in the territory of the Federal Republic of Germany, because descendants of members of the Polish Union still live in the Federal Republic of Germany. How and when Mr. President Mr. Minister, as the case may be. It intends to remove from the text of the Polish-German Treaty this apparent lack of symmetry, which results in the negative phenomena described above for Poles in Germany, but also in the unequal status of Poland towards Germany? When will the Polish authorities, responsible for its foreign policy, finally rise from their knees and lead to a state of change of the Treaty in accordance with the Polish law? As regards the implementation of the Treaty, it is Article. 20 in the remaining paragraphs, from 2 to 4, and Article. 21, despite different terms, give, at least on a blank basis, the same rights to Poles in Germany and Germany in Poland. But only on paper. In fact, aid to Poles residing in Germany, granted by the German state, is a small fraction of what Germany has obtained and obtained in Poland, despite the much larger number of German Polonia and the economic strength of the Federal Republic of Germany. It is all the more surprising that the German government is reluctant to comply with the treaty provisions, planned and purposefully constantly ignoring the Treaty provisions, covered with empty promises and phrases about Polish-German reconciliation and the community of interests. How and when Mr. President Mr. Minister, as the case may be. Will it lead to the full implementation by Germany of the provisions signed by them and ratified by the Treaty? What tools and international institutions do you intend to use for this purpose, especially since the present twenty-fiveth anniversary of the signing of the Treaty is a great opportunity to review the implementation of its provisions? Instead of the ritual and obscuring reality of the officials, politically correct ceremonies proving the epic friendship of the German-Polish people, I propose a clear formulation of our Polish interests and consistent with the spirit and letter of the treaty their enforcement. I'd like to express my respects. Jerzy Czerwiński, Senator of the Republic of Poland.
